## AI Research

### [Extracting Performant Algorithms Using Mechanistic Interpretability](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R4xxxAfNpAvpb3LCf/extracting-performant-algorithms-using-mechanistic-5)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span>A Prequel: The Tree of Life Inside a DNA Language Model</span></h2><p><span>Last year, researchers at Goodfire AI </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/phylogeny-manifold" rel="noreferrer"><span>took</span></a><span> Evo 2, a genomic foundation model, and found, quite literally, the </span><i><span>evolutionary</span></i><span> </span><i><span>tree of life </span></i><span>inside. The phylogenetic relationships between thousands of species were encoded as a curved manifold in the model's internal activations, with geodesic distances along that manifold tracking actual evolutionary branch lengths. Bacteria that diverged hundreds of millions of years ago were far apart on the manifold, and closely related species were nearby. </span></p><p><span>The model was trained to predict the next DNA token. Nobody told it about evolution or gave it a phylogenetic tree as a training signal. But the model needed to encode evolutionary relationships in order to predict DNA well, and so it built a structured geometric representation of those relationships as part of its internal computation, and the representation was good enough that you could extract it with interpretability tools and compare it meaningfully to the ground truth.</span></p><p><span>I saw this and decided to apply the same approach to another type of biological foundation models - </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s12276-025-01547-5"><span>those trained on single cell data</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>If Evo 2 learned the tree of life from raw DNA, what did </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41592-024-02201-0"><span>scGPT</span></a><span> learn about how human cells develop?</span></p><h2><span>Finding the Manifold</span></h2><p><span>For those unfamiliar with the biology side: scGPT is a transformer model trained on millions of single-cell gene expression profiles. Each cell in your body expresses thousands of genes at varying levels, and a single-cell RNA sequencing experiment measures those expression levels for potentially hundreds of thousands of individual cells simultaneously. scGPT was pre-trained on this kind of data in a generative fashion, learning to predict masked gene expression values from context. </span></p><p><span>The question I wanted to answer was: does scGPT encode, somewhere in its attention tensor, a compact geometric representation of some biological processes? And if so, can I find it without knowing in advance exactly where to look?</span></p><p><span>To attack this systematically, I used a two-phase research loop driven by an AI executor-reviewer pair operating under pre-registered quality gates. Phase 1 was a broad hypothesis search: the loop explored a large combinatorial space of candidate manifold hypotheses by varying the biological target (developmental ordering, regulatory structure, communication geometry), the featurization strategy (attention drift, raw embeddings, mixed operators), and the geometric fitting method (Isomap, geodesic MDS, a technique called Locally Euclidean Transformations), all applied across the full 12-layer × 8-head scGPT attention tensor, which means 96 individual attention units to screen. </span></p><p><span>What came out of Phase 1 was a robust positive hit: hypothesis H65, which identified a compact, roughly 8-to-10-dimensional manifold in specific attention heads where positions along the manifold corresponded to how far cells had progressed through hematopoietic differentiation. Stem cells clustered at one end; terminally differentiated blood cell types (T cells, B cells, monocytes, macrophages) spread out along distinct branches at the other end; and the branching topology matched the known developmental hierarchy with statistically significant branch structure that held up under stringent controls.</span></p><p><span>Then I switched to Phase 2 which was rather manual investigation: methodological closure tests, confidence intervals, structured holdouts, and external validation. I validated the manifold on a non-overlapping panel from </span><a href="https://tabula-sapiens.sf.czbiohub.org"><span>Tabula Sapiens</span></a><span> and then confirmed it via frozen-head zero-shot transfer to an entirely independent multi-donor immune panel. You can explore this manifold yourself and compare different extraction variants, in an </span><a href="https://biodyn-ai.github.io/hema-manifold/"><span>interactive 3D viewer</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>But Does the Extracted Algorithm Actually Work?</span></h2><p><span>I think finding a biologically meaningful manifold inside a foundation model is, on its own, cool. But the question I actually cared about was: can you take this geometric object </span><i><span>out</span></i><span> of the model and use it as a standalone method that does useful work?</span></p><p><span>To do it, I developed a three-stage extraction pipeline:</span></p><ol><li><span>I directly exported the frozen attention weight matrices from the relevant heads, with no retraining, just literally reading out the learned linear operator.</span></li><li><span> I attached a lightweight learned adaptor that projects the raw attention output into the manifold's coordinate system. </span></li><li><span>And then I added a task-specific readout head (for classification or pseudotime prediction). </span></li></ol><p><span>The key property of this pipeline is that the heavy lifting, the actual biological knowledge, comes entirely from the frozen attention weights that scGPT learned during pre-training. The adaptor and readout are small and cheap to train, and they never touch the original dataset the model was pre-trained on. What you end up with is a standalone algorithm you can ship as a file and run independently of scGPT.</span></p><p><span>So: how does it perform?</span></p><p><span>I benchmarked the extracted algorithm against a lineup of established methods that biologists actually use in practice: scVI (a deep generative model for single-cell data), Palantir (a pseudotime method based on diffusion maps and Markov chains), Diffusion Pseudotime (the Scanpy implementation), CellTypist (a logistic-regression-based cell type classifier trained on a large reference atlas), PCA, and raw-expression baselines. These are the standard tools in the single-cell bioinformatics toolkit, developed and refined by domain experts over years.</span></p><p><span>On pseudotime-depth ordering, which measures how well a method recovers the true developmental progression from stem cells to mature blood cells, the extracted algorithm appeared to be </span><b><span>the best, significantly outperforming every tested alternative in paired split-level statistics</span></b><span>. On classification (distinguishing cell types), the picture was less unambiguous but still strong: the extracted head led on branch balanced accuracy and on key subtype discrimination tasks like CD4/CD8 T cell separation and monocyte/macrophage distinction. On some stage-level and branch-level macro-F1 metrics, diffusion-style baselines or raw expression had the edge, so this is not a clean sweep, but the extracted algorithm is solidly in the top tier across the board, and dominant on the most biologically meaningful endpoint.</span></p><p><span>Now, you might reasonably ask: is this just the result of having a fancier probe? Maybe any sufficiently flexible function fitted on top of scGPT's embeddings would do equally well, and the "manifold discovery" part is not contributing anything real. I tested this. A 3-layer MLP with 175,000 trainable parameters, fitted on frozen scGPT average-pooled embeddings, was significantly </span><i><span>worse</span></i><span> than the extracted 10-dimensional head on 6 out of 8 classification endpoints. And the extracted head accomplished this </span><b><span>while being 34.5 times faster </span></b><span>to evaluate across a full 12-split campaign, </span><b><span>with roughly 1,000 times fewer trainable parameters</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Let me restate this: </span><b><span>the geometric structure that mechanistic interpretability found inside scGPT's attention heads, when extracted and used directly, outperforms the standard approach of slapping an MLP on top of the model's embeddings. The interpretability-derived method is simultaneously more accurate, faster, and smaller. </span></b></p><h2><span>How Small Can You Go Though?</span></h2><p><span>Once you have an extracted algorithm that works, the natural next question is how much of it you actually need. Compression is interesting for practical reasons, but it is even more interesting for interpretability reasons, because the further you compress an algorithm while preserving its performance, the closer you get to understanding what it is actually doing.</span></p><p><span>The initial extracted operator pooled three attention heads from scGPT and weighed 17.5 MB. Not large by modern standards, but not trivially inspectable either. The first compression step was to ask: do we really need all three heads, or does a single one carry the essential geometry? I scanned all 96 attention units in scGPT's tensor and found that a single unit, Layer 2, Head 5, carried substantial transferable developmental geometry on its own. The compact operator built from this single head weighed 5.9 MB and showed almost no loss compared to the three-head version on the benchmark suite. </span></p><p><span>The second compression step was more aggressive: truncated SVD on the single-head operator. This factors the weight matrix into low-rank components and throws away everything below a chosen rank threshold. At rank 64, the resulting surrogate shrinks to 0.73 MB, which is already quite tiny, and it still beats the frozen scGPT average-pool + MLP baseline on all eight pooled classification endpoints. It does incur statistically significant losses versus the dense single-head operator on 5 out of 8 endpoints, so this is not free compression. But the rank-64 version is still a better algorithm than the standard probing approach, </span><b><span>at a fraction of a megabyte</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>And also, now the interpretability payoff arrives. I ran a factor ablation audit on the rank-64 surrogate: systematically remove each of the 64 factors one at a time, measure how much performance drops, and rank them by necessity. And it appeared that just four factors, out of 64, accounted for 66% of the total pooled ablation impact. And then, when I examined what those four factors corresponded to biologically, they resolved into explicit hematopoietic gene programs.</span></p><h2><span>So, Mechanistic Interpretability is Becoming Dual Use</span></h2><p><span>Let's step back from the specific results for a moment and consider the high-level lesson here.</span></p><p><span>The very property that makes this result interesting is also the property that makes me cautious about applying the same techniques to large language models. Because the argument runs in both directions. If you can extract an algorithm that a model uses to do something well, you can potentially also </span><i><span>improve</span></i><span> how it does that thing: by identifying inefficient components, scaling the relevant circuits, composing extracted subroutines in new ways, by replacing the fuzzy learned version with a cleaner extracted version and freeing up capacity for the model to learn something else etc. Mechanistic interpretability, in other words, is becoming a </span><i><span>capability amplification</span></i><span> tool. This is a well-known theoretical concern, but it looks like now it is becoming a practical one.</span></p><p><span>Consider a few scenarios. You identify the circuit in a language model responsible for multi-step planning, extract it, find that it is operating at low rank with substantial redundancy, and publish a paper showing how to compress it. Now anyone training the next generation of models can initialize that circuit more efficiently, or allocate more capacity to the components that matter. Or: you discover that a model's chain-of-thought reasoning relies on a specific attention pattern that routes information through intermediate tokens in a predictable way, and you publish a detailed mechanistic account of how this works. Now someone building an inference-time scaling pipeline can optimize that routing directly rather than relying on the model to rediscover it from scratch. </span></p><p><span>This is one of the reasons why I have deliberately chosen to focus my interpretability work on biological foundation models. Although I agree that pushing biology can also be associated with risks, I believe that we really need to push biology asap, considering the current AI risks landscape, and pushing biology is what I am trying to do.</span></p><h2><span>Mechanistic Intepretability for Novel Knowledge Discovery</span></h2><p><span>On a more positive and general note: on top of being an auditing/monitoring tool, mechanistic interpretability can be a </span><i><span>knowledge</span></i><span> </span><i><span>discovery</span></i><span> tool. Consider:</span></p><ol><li><span>The model learned something about hematopoiesis that existing bioinformatics methods had not fully captured, at least not in the same compact form. </span></li><li><span>The interpretability pipeline found a representation that, when extracted and deployed as a standalone algorithm, outperformed established tools on the most biologically meaningful benchmarks. </span></li><li><span>The knowledge extracted from the model's internals was </span><i><span>new</span></i><span> in the operationally relevant sense: nobody had this particular algorithm before, and it works better than what people were using.</span></li></ol><h2><span>Join In</span></h2><p><span>If you like mechanistic interpetability, I encourage you to consider switching from LLMs to biological foundation models.</span></p><p><span>The work I described here is part of a broader research program on mechanistic interpretability of biological foundation models. Earlier I published a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SjWCbyWLqBLoP6WtJ/mechanistic-interpretability-of-biological-foundation-models"><span>comprehensive stress-test of attention-based interpretability</span></a><span> methods on scGPT and Geneformer. In parallel, I developed a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02952"><span>sparse autoencoder atlas</span></a><span> covering 107,000+ features across all layers of both Geneformer and scGPT. The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10261"><span>hematopoietic manifold paper</span></a><span> is the latest piece.</span></p><p><span>There is a lot more to do here, both in terms of applying these methods to other biological systems and developmental processes, and in terms of developing better unsupervised techniques for manifold discovery that could scale beyond what the current semi-supervised approach allows. I think this is one of the best places in the current research landscape to do interpretability work that is simultaneously methodologically interesting, practically useful for biomedicine (and yes, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zvu53dBJEwenovkE2/overview-of-some-biotech-based-adult-intelligence"><span>human intelligence amplification</span></a><span>), and safe with respect to the capability externalities that worry me about LLM interpretability.</span></p><p><span>You can find more about the research program, ongoing projects, and ways to get involved at </span><a href="https://biodynai.com/"><span>biodynai.com</span></a><span>. The full paper with all supplementary materials is on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10261"><span>arXiv</span></a><span>, and the interactive 3D manifold viewer is at </span><a href="https://biodyn-ai.github.io/hema-manifold/"><span>biodyn-ai.github.io/hema-manifold</span></a><span>.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R4xxxAfNpAvpb3LCf/extracting-performant-algorithms-using-mechanistic-5#comments">Discuss</a>

### [New LessWrong Editor! (Also, an update to our LLM policy.)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nQWavk9mnwcv6ScMR/new-lesswrong-editor-also-an-update-to-our-llm-policy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>There's a new editor experience on LessWrong! A bunch of the editor page has been rearranged to make it much more WYSIWYG compared to published post pages. All of the settings live in panels that are hidden by default and can be opened up by clicking the relevant buttons on the side of the screen. We also adopted lexical as a new editor framework powering everything behind the scenes (we were previously using ckEditor).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1773433647/lexical_client_uploads/mwxl0xjswdu7vlubjxzh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>That scary arrow button in the top-left doesn't publish your post!  It just opens the publishing menu.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Posts</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefor44zdzw0tp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnor44zdzw0tp">[1]</a></sup></span><span> now have automatic real-time autosave while you're online (like Google Docs), but still support offline editing if your connection drops out.  Point-in-time revisions will still get autosaved periodically, and you can always manually save your draft if you want a specific checkpoint.</span></p><p><span>The editor also has a slash menu now!</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 20.84592145015106%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1773348116/lexical_client_uploads/adeo2xmststgotgztshj.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Good for </span><s><span>all</span></s><span> many of your custom content needs!</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>You might be eyeing the last two items in that slash menu.  This post will demo some of the new features, and I'll demo two of them simultaneously by letting Opus 4.6 explain what they are:</span></p><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><p><span>Hi! I'm Claude, and I'm writing this from inside the post you're reading right now. This block I'm in is one of the new editor features — let me walk you through a few of them.</span></p><h3><span>LLM Content Blocks</span></h3><p><span>This visually distinct block is an </span><b><span>LLM Content Block</span></b><span>. Authors can insert these into their posts to clearly attribute a section to a specific AI model. The block header shows which model generated the content, so readers always know what they're looking at. It's a way to be transparent about AI-assisted writing while keeping everything in one document.</span></p><h3><span>Custom Iframe Widgets</span></h3><p><span>The new editor supports </span><b><span>custom interactive widgets</span></b><span> embedded directly in posts. Authors can write HTML and JavaScript that runs in a sandboxed iframe right in the document — useful for interactive demos, visualizations, small tools, or anything else that benefits from being more than static text. There's one just below this block, in fact.</span></p><h3><span>Agent Integration</span></h3><p><span>The editor now has an API that lets AI agents read and edit drafts collaboratively. If you share your draft's edit link with an AI assistant (like me), it can insert text, leave Google Docs-style comments, make suggested edits, and add LLM content blocks and widgets — all showing up live in the editor. That's how this entire block was written: not copy-pasted in, but inserted directly through the API while the post was open for editing.</span></p><p><span>To use it, open your post's sharing settings and set "Anyone with the link can" to Edit, then copy the edit URL and share it with your AI assistant.</span></p><p><span>With Edit permissions, the agent can do everything: insert and modify text, add widgets, create LLM content blocks, and more. If you'd prefer to keep tighter control, </span><b><span>Comment</span></b><span> permissions still allow the agent to leave inline comments and suggested edits, which you can accept or reject individually.</span></p><p><span>Setup depends on which AI tool you're using. Agent harnesses that can make HTTP requests directly — like Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor — should work out of the box. If you're using </span><b><span>Claude on </span></b><a href="https://claude.ai" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>claude.ai</span></b></a><span>, you'll need to add </span><code><span>www.lesswrong.com</span></code><span> to your </span><a href="https://claude.ai/settings/capabilities"><span>allowed domains settings</span></a><span>, then start a new chat. (The ChatGPT web UI doesn't currently support whitelisting external domains, so it can't be used for this feature yet.) Once that's done, just paste your edit URL and ask Claude to read the post — the API is self-describing, so it'll figure out the rest from there.</span></p></div></div><p><span>And here's a </span><span>small interactive widget</span><span>, also written by Claude</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffzo5um3byng"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfzo5um3byng">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, to demonstrate custom iframe widgets:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><br /></p><h1><span>Policy on LLM Use</span></h1><p><span>You might be wondering what this means for our policy on LLM use.</span></p><p><span>Our initial policy was this:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>A rough guideline is that if you are using AI for writing assistance, you should spend a minimum of 1 minute per 50 words (enough to read the content several times and perform significant edits), you should not include any information that you can't verify, haven't verified, or don't understand, and you should not use the stereotypical writing style of an AI assistant.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>You were also permitted to put LLM-generated content into collapsible sections, if you labeled it as LLM-generated.</span></p><p><span>In practice, the "you should not use the stereotypical writing style of an AI assistant" part of the requirement meant that this was a de-facto ban on LLM use, which we enforced mostly consistently on new users and very inconsistently on existing users</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs43frbsc5jb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns43frbsc5jb">[3]</a></sup></span><span>.  Bad!</span></p><p><span>To motivate our updated policy, we must first do some philosophy.  Why do we care about knowing whether something we're reading was generated by an LLM?  </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DDG2Tf2sqc8rTWRk3/llm-generated-text-is-not-testimony" rel="noreferrer"><span>LLM-generated text is not testimony</span></a><span> has substantially informed my thinking on this question.  Take the synopsis:</span></p><blockquote><ol><li><span>When we share words with each other, we don't only care about the words themselves. We care also—even primarily—about the mental elements of the human mind/agency that produced the words. What we want to engage with is those mental elements.</span></li><li><span>As of 2025, LLM text does not have those elements behind it.</span></li><li><span>Therefore&nbsp;LLM text categorically does not serve the role for communication that is served by real text.</span></li><li><span>Therefore the norm should be that you don't share LLM text as if someone wrote it. And, it is inadvisable to read LLM text that someone else shares as though someone wrote it.</span></li></ol></blockquote><p><span>I don't think you even need to confidently believe in point 2</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref09tu8uhs95tm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn09tu8uhs95tm">[4]</a></sup></span><span> for the norm in point 4 to be compelling.  It is merely sufficient that </span><i><span>someone else</span></i><span> produced the text.</span></p><p><span>Plagiarism is often considered bad because it's "stealing credit" for someone else's work.  But it's also bad because it's misinforming your readers about your beliefs and mental models!  What happens if someone asks you why you're so confident about [proposition X]?  It really sucks if the answer is "Oh, uh, I didn't write that sentence, and re-reading it, it turns out I'm not actually </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> confident in that claim..."</span></p><p><span>This is also why having LLMs "edit" your writing is often pernicious. LLM editing, unless managed extremely carefully, often involves rephrasings, added qualifiers, and swapped vocabulary in ways that meaningfully change the semantic content of your writing. Very often this is in unendorsed ways, but this can be hard to pick up on because the typical LLM writing style has a tendency to make people's eyes slide off of it</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftwnpwaeibe"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntwnpwaeibe">[5]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>With all that in mind, our new policy is this:</span></p><ul><li><b><span>"LLM output"</span></b><span> includes all of:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item"><ul><li><span>text written entirely by an LLM</span></li><li><span>text that was written by a human and then substantially</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref87euids1664"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn87euids1664">[6]</a></sup></span><span> edited or revised by an LLM</span></li><li><span>text that was written by an LLM and then edited or revised by a human</span></li></ul></li><li><span>"LLM output" does not include:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item"><ul><li><span>text that was written by a human and then</span><span> lightly edited or revised by an LLM</span></li><li><span>text written by a human, which includes facts, arguments, examples, etc, which were researched/discovered/developed with LLM assistance.  (If you "borrow language" from the LLM, that no longer counts as "text written by a human".)</span></li><li><span>code (either in code blocks or in the new widgets)</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>"LLM output" must go into the new LLM content blocks.  You can put "LLM output" into a collapsible section without wrapping it in an LLM content block if all of the content is "LLM output".  If it's mixed, you should use LLM content blocks within the collapsible section to demarcate those parts which are "LLM output". </span></p><p><span>We are going to be more strictly enforcing the "no LLM output" rule by normalizing our auto-moderation logic to treat posts by approved</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqcbd160asy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqcbd160asy">[7]</a></sup></span><span> users similarly to posts by new users - that is, they'll be automatically rejected if they score above a certain threshold in our automated LLM content detection pipeline. Having spent a few months staring at what's been coming down the pipe, we are also going to be lowering that threshold.</span></p><p><span>This does not change our existing quality bar for new user submissions.  If you are a new user and submit a post that substantially consists of content inside of LLM content blocks, it is pretty unlikely that it will get approved</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref71sigspfz4y"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn71sigspfz4y">[8]</a></sup></span><span>. This does not suddenly become wise if you're an approved user.  If you're confident that people will want to read it, then sure, go ahead, but please pay close attention to the kind of feedback you get (karma, comments, etc), and if this proves noisy we'll probably just tell people to cut it out.</span></p><hr /><p><span>As always, please submit feedback, questions, and bug reports via Intercom (or in the comments below, if you prefer).</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnor44zdzw0tp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefor44zdzw0tp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Not comments or other content types that use the editor, like tags - those still have the same local backup mechanism they've always had, and you can still explicitly save draft comments, but none of them get automatically synced to the cloud as you type.  Also, existing posts and drafts will continue to use the previous editor, and won't have access to the new features.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfzo5um3byng"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffzo5um3byng">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Prompted by </span><a href="http://localhost:3000/users/jimrandomh?mention=user"><span>@jimrandomh</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns43frbsc5jb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs43frbsc5jb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For somewhat contingent reasons involving various choices we made with our moderation setup.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn09tu8uhs95tm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref09tu8uhs95tm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See my </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DDG2Tf2sqc8rTWRk3/llm-generated-text-is-not-testimony?commentId=L3robhc3TrTZcdPRf" rel="noreferrer"><span>curation notice</span></a><span> on that post for some additional thoughts and caveats.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntwnpwaeibe"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftwnpwaeibe">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jL7uDE5oH4HddYq4u/raemon-s-shortform?commentId=DZToM4tksLvsCRvrB" rel="noreferrer"><span>this recent thread</span></a><span> is </span><span>instructive</span><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn87euids1664"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a ### [Probably you won't be able to perform a data-driven habit stacking for self-improvement](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/probably-you-won-t-be-able-to-perform-a-data-driven-habit)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Suppose you’re not happy with the quality of your sleep. You’ve already stopped doing the obviously harmful things (no more coffee at night), and your sleep has improved - but you’d like to work on it further. A coworker gives you an herbal mix with St. John’s wort and lavender. You try drinking it at night instead of coffee, and it does seem that sometimes your sleep really does get deeper than before. But sometimes it doesn’t. You’re willing to experiment, but how do you actually check whether the herbs work, or whether it’s just random variation?</p><p>Or suppose you’re not particularly satisfied with your productivity at work. Following the advice from "Atomic Habits" and books on workflow organization, you’ve introduced a few useful micro‑habits and ergonomic improvements. But what do you do once the low‑hanging fruits are picked up? Time is limited - you can’t implement everything that someone somewhere calls "useful". Some habits are even mutually exclusive: it’s impossible to both socialize during lunch and sit alone in silence at the same time.</p><p>Or, for example, you want to achieve better results in fishing… you get the idea.</p><p>"Don’t underestimate the power of small things taken in large numbers" is a wise thought - but how do you figure out which small things actually work in your specific case? This is quite a roadblock on the path to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DoLQN5ryZ9XkZjq5h/tsuyoku-naritai-i-want-to-become-stronger">becoming stronger</a>. If you’ve taken a statistics course or have heard the term "A/B testing," you probably already have a rough outline of an answer. Pick a target metric, collect historical data, then collect data after the chosen intervention, compare the average values before and after - and voilà, a scientifically grounded answer. The problem is that if you jump into a project like this without a solid plan, you’ll likely end up with a pile of useless numbers in a spreadsheet. Why this kind of endeavor is not for the faint‑hearted is exactly what I want to talk about in this article.</p><p><strong>TLDR</strong>: Most effects you’re likely to encounter are too weak to detect against the background noise of everyday life. Even for a relatively noticeable effect, you need a carefully thought‑out experimental design and several months of work.</p><h1>Notation</h1><p>The following notation is used throughout the text:</p><ul><li>D - variance (a measure of how spread out a random variable is)</li><li><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;- standard deviation (the square root of variance)</li><li><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;- a normal distribution with mean&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and variance D</li><li><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;- Cohen’s d statistic (the distance between the peaks of two normal-distribution populations, normalized by the square root of the sum of their variances)</li></ul><p>For simplicity, the text below assumes that the intervention does not change the shape of the distribution (i.e. d is measured in units of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;of the original distribution). This does not strongly affect the arguments below. I’m also aware of the limitations of using the normal distribution for modeling. If we take those limitations seriously, the outlook for self‑experimentation becomes <i>even</i> <i>worse</i>.</p><h1>Minimal background for people who want to read the article but don’t know probability theory</h1><p>Skip this section if you know what the symbols above mean and remember the basic properties of the normal distribution.</p><p>There will be no detailed derivations or rigorous proofs here. The article is far too short for me to teach even an introductory statistics course. But here’s a short summary so that readers unfamiliar with statistics can still follow the reasoning that comes next.</p><p>Imagine a regular six‑sided die. If it’s a fair die, the probability of rolling any given face is 1/6 = 16.66%. This is a uniform distribution - all outcomes are equally likely. It can be visualized with the following histogram:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/ycdfc1j0e21rcpmtplbc" /></figure><p>The horizontal axis shows the outcomes, the vertical axis shows their probabilities. The sum of probabilities over all outcomes is equal to one (100%).</p><p>But if we roll two six‑sided dice, the distribution of the <i>sum</i> of the outcomes is no longer uniform. If we list all possible outcomes:</p><ul><li>1 + 1 = 2</li><li>1 + 2 = 3</li><li>2 + 1 = 3</li><li>1 + 3 = 4</li><li>2 + 2 = 4</li><li>3 + 1 = 4</li><li>1 + 4 = 5</li><li>...</li><li>5 + 6 = 11</li><li>6 + 5 = 11</li><li>6 + 6 = 12</li></ul><p>we can see that 2 can only occur if both dice show 1 (which happens in 1 out of 36 cases). A sum of 3 can happen in two ways: the first die is 1 and the second is 2, or vice versa - so the probability is 2/36. The chance of rolling a 4 is even higher: 3/36, and so on. The largest number of combinations produce a sum of 7. These include cases where both dice land near the middle of the range (3+4, 4+3), as well as cases where a small value is compensated by a large one (1+6, 2+5, 5+2, 6+1).</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/rwpzbi45hofxzljkwasx" /></figure><p>The bars of the histogram form a triangle. What happens if we roll three six‑sided dice instead?</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/jlizzp6nk5yljwvk6md1" /></figure><p>You can see that the center becomes smoother, while the tails spread out. Again, this happens because getting a 3 or an 18 requires all three dice to land on 1 or 6 respectively. Central values, on the other hand, can arise both when all dice land near the middle and when small values compensate large ones.</p><p>If we take 25 dice, the resulting plot takes a particular bell shape:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/oeia4g12roufzhlsut9o" /></figure><p>For practical purposes, a curve like this is already quite close to a <strong>normal distribution</strong>. Many things in our lives follow an (approximately) normal distribution: the height of men of a fixed age in a given country, measurement errors of most instruments, your mood over the course of a year. This happens for the same reason as with the sum of dice. You’re in a good mood when everything goes according to plan (all dice roll 5–6). You’re in a bad mood when nothing works out (all dice roll 1–2). But an average mood can result both from an unremarkable day and from good things compensating for bad ones.</p><p>("Didn’t get enough sleep this night. But I remembered my favorite school teacher in the morning, which gave me a motivational boost. Then there was a traffic jam on the way to work - so irritating! At least the day is sunny; you can feel that spring is coming - I love spring.")</p><p>Probability theory says that the elements being summed don’t have to be uniformly distributed. As long as they have a sufficiently "nice" shape, their sum will converge to a normal distribution. This is called the central limit theorem. And "niceness" holds surprisingly often.</p><p>The bell curve can have different widths, and we’d like to be able to measure this spread. Simply measuring the distance between the leftmost and rightmost values is impractical, because values near the edges of the bell occur very rarely. So in practice, we use variance (<span class="math-tex"></span>) - the mean squared distance from the center of the distribution - or standard deviation (<span class="math-tex"></span>), the square root of variance.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/fft3jafqkksmcftqfdxu" /></figure><p>The red arrows in the picture show distances from the center to several points. To compute the standard deviation, we take all these distances, square each of them, sum them up, divide by their count, and then take the square root of the result.</p><p>It is known that 68% of elements of a normal distribution lie within the range&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;, and 95% lie within&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Conversely, if we know that 95% of the elements of a normal distribution fall within a certain range, we can <i>estimate</i>&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/koxassc3ab6c2joh4opu" /></figure><p>Now suppose we have two sets of values: an original random distribution and the same distribution shifted by some amount. For example, a database of how well students performed on a test after 48 weeks of studying a subject, and how well they performed after 96 weeks. How do we estimate how much doubling the time improves the results? It’s convenient to measure this shift in units of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>: this way of measuring reflects not the absolute change, but the change relative to the natural variability. A ten‑point improvement can be impressive if the typical spread is five points, and almost meaningless if the spread is fifty points.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/tdtzyhhiwovojpbh2tvs" /></figure><p>What if the intervention not only shifts the mean, but also increases the spread of values? That’s not a problem - we take the average&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;according to a standard rule:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and measure the distance in those units:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span></p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/hpawop9puwg0xjovtzah" /></figure><p>This is called Cohen’s d statistic. Scientific papers usually report results using exactly this measure.</p><h1>How large of effects should you expect from self‑experimentation?</h1><p>It’s hard to answer this question in general. Some people are trying to optimize sleep quality, others are aiming to gain muscles. Let’s calibrate expectations - here are Cohen’s d values for a few well‑studied effects:</p><ul><li>Taking creatine (a dietary supplement widely used among bodybuilders), depending on the protocol and how outcomes are measured, yields an additional performance gain of about d = 0.27–0.59<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwffp80j9ywk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwffp80j9ywk">[1]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>Strength training with a coach, as opposed to training alone, adds another d = 0.28–0.4 depending on how performance is measured<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref81xsjgiyuu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn81xsjgiyuu">[2]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>Meta‑analyses estimate the average effect of antidepressants at around d = 0.3<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9ozrkou5rpg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9ozrkou5rpg">[3]</a></sup></span>. The effect is smaller in cases of mild depression. In real life, however, one should expect a much larger impact: a doctor can try several medications and fine‑tune the dosage.</li><li>There is no consensus on the effectiveness of mindfulness meditation for treating anxiety disorders. Studies show a wide spread of results, but an average estimate of d = 0.2 seems quite plausible<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefamiz7wg0i9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnamiz7wg0i9">[4]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9x2iafcvf4u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9x2iafcvf4u">[5]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>Moderate‑intensity strength training (60–80% of one‑rep max) reduces glycated hemoglobin levels by about d = 0.4<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3x1u7anakux"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3x1u7anakux">[6]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>Additional financial education classes yield d = 0.15 for financial knowledge (test scores) and d = 0.07 for financially responsible behavior (for example, building a larger emergency fund)<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqj2h5omoib"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqj2h5omoib">[7]</a></sup></span>.</li></ul><p>On the one hand, your personal life situation or biochemistry might amplify effects that are useless for most people. On top of that, studies are limited by the fact that the same "dose" of an intervention is applied to all participants, whereas you can fine‑tune the "dose" much more precisely for yourself. On the other hand, you’re unlikely to test anything on yourself that’s stronger than prescription antidepressants. Also, most likely you’ll want to optimize things like "mood" or "energy throughout the day." It’s rare for something to simultaneously affect biology, externally imposed daily schedules, psychology, and the habits feeding into these metrics. So expecting effects of d &gt; 1 from any intervention in these cases would be strange. Either you already know about such interventions and are already doing them (you’ve almost certainly gone to school - not some specific course, but school in general). Or you know about them but aren’t doing them for good reasons (moving to a Buddhist monastery would almost certainly have a strong effect on anxiety but there are legitimate reasons not to do that). Or these are major life‑changing decisions that don’t just happen casually, and that are hard to reproduce and study - leaving an abusive partner, or radically changing career track.</p><p>As a result, in my opinion, if you’re trying to optimize your life by tweaking habits and selecting supplements, you’re effectively "hunting" for effects in the range d = 0.1–0.4.</p><h1>What does d = 0.1–0.4 actually mean in real life?</h1><ul><li><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in IQ measurement is about 1.5 IQ points. An extra 1 centimeter of height corresponds to roughly&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in the distribution of adult male height<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiq5me4v4f4e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniq5me4v4f4e">[8]</a></sup></span>. Not much - but I wouldn’t casually throw away extra IQ points or height centimeters.</li><li>The 10th percentile of electric motor assemblers in the US earns about $25k per year, while the 90th percentile earns $56.5k<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9x3w0mm1mmk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9x3w0mm1mmk">[9]</a></sup></span>. Roughly speaking, the standard deviation of this distribution is around $8.5k per year, and 0.2 of that is $1.7k per year. That’s enough to buy a decent bicycle in Texas.</li><li>If someone has between 5 and 45 free hours per week for leisure in 95% of cases, then an effect that increases free time by&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;would give them an extra 3 hours per week. Again, not a huge amount - but enough to play one new board game per week, or to finish Dispatch in three weeks<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnun2d0eulc8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnun2d0eulc8">[10]</a></sup></span>.</li><li>If in 95% of cases you read 40–160 pages per week (a 400‑page book per month), then&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;corresponds to an extra 12 pages per week (about one and a half additional 400‑page books per year).</li></ul><p>In medical literature, effects of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;are considered "small,"&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;- "medium," and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;- "large"<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgl9d612ajkp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngl9d612ajkp">[11]</a></sup></span>. The examples above make it clear why:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;really isn’t that much. But let’s remember the context. Medical literature evaluates drugs against specific diseases. You can’t give patients arbitrary doses of whatever you want. Doctors have to justify why they prescribe a particular pill, because side effects are real. In your own life, on the other hand, nobody stops you from applying as many interventions as you want - until you run out of money or free time. If, in the free‑time example, you could find and apply three such formally "small" effects, that would give you 9 extra free hours per week. That’s a full workday! So the idea of stacking many small effects is at least worth a serious look.</p><p>Sounds tempting - but then what’s the catch?</p><h1>How many observations, exactly?</h1><p>To estimate the absolute effect of an intervention, we take a set of observations before the intervention and another set after it, and then compare the mean values of the two sets. The problem is that we need a lot of data. For example, in the figure below we have only 4 observations - and we got unlucky. Even though the green distribution is actually <i>to the right</i> of the blue one, based on our limited data we would have to conclude that it’s <i>to the left</i>, and by quite a lot:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/t24gs8jvmg9wnk5tnpeg" /></figure><p>(There’s a similar issue when estimating the standard deviation needed to compute Cohen’s d, but let’s focus on the means for now.) All we can hope for is that with more observations, the chance of getting unlucky with <i>all</i> of them decreases. For example, here some of the variability partially cancels out:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/ao5zbcsnbcolcgsgxsed" /></figure><p>Computing the mean difference for the plot above gives&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, which is closer to the true shift of 0.25 - but still not that close.</p><p>The pooled standard deviation, computed using the method from the previous section, is equal to 1, so the d‑value is also 0.6:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span></p><p>So how many observations do we actually need to be confident? Let’s look at how well the estimated value converges to the true effect. Below are plots from twenty simulations showing how our estimate of the difference between two normal distributions with D = 1 depends on the number of observations. We’re trying to detect true effects of d = 0.1, d = 0.2, and d = 0.3, respectively:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/iwwf8uaobtkwoieffhxj" /></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/oedfqf1fdhwzmz8rcjtw" /></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/xkxwmhumsrbhjyazm15w" /></figure><p>From these plots we can draw several conclusions:</p><ul><li><i>On average</i>, we do converge to the true effect. The more observations we have, the closer we get.</li><li>That said, you don’t know in advance which simulation branch you’re on. You can either underestimate or overestimate the effect of the intervention.</li><li>The spread of the estimates shrinks relatively quickly at first, but after about 200 observations, adding more data barely reduces the spread. At the given noise level, it takes around 1200 observations for the simulation curves to consistently fall within a&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;band around the true value.</li><li>The convergence rate depends only very weakly on the true effect size.</li><li>This isn’t visible in the plots, but the single most important factor is the noise level - the natural variability of the observed quantity.</li></ul><p>If you look closely at the plots, you’ll see that even for an effect of d = 0.3, you need more than 175 observations before <i>all</i> simulation runs at least register a positive effect. For d = 0.1, you need roughly 1000. But even this understates how hard the experiment really is! In practice, we need to distinguish between interventions that have an effect and interventions that have no effect at all. That means the bundles of simulations for "effect" and "no effect" need to be sufficiently separated for us to tell which bundle our observed line belongs to:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/xxyrx6zyyzrmel5vtgrd" /></figure><p>From the image above, it’s clear that separating these bundles requires around 600–700 observations.</p><p>The plots are intuitive, but not very formal. What does theory say?</p><h1>p‑value</h1><p>In science, the standard way to assess whether we can reject the null hypothesis (no effect; the intervention has no impact) is via a p‑value test. The p‑value for an effect is the probability that the null hypothesis is true <strong>and</strong> that we observe a difference between two datasets that is <i>at least</i> as large as the one we got. Conventionally, if this probability is below 5%, we reject the null hypothesis. This is the well‑known criterion&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>How can we estimate the probability of observing the measured difference if there is actually no effect? Once again, the normal distribution comes to the rescue. The sum and the difference of normally distributed random variables are themselves normally distributed<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhhn0z7hisc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhhn0z7hisc">[12]</a></sup></span>. As a result, the difference of sample means&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is also normally distributed. If the null hypothesis is true and the distributions&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;are identical, then the peak of this difference distribution is at zero<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5lw9afvjsvj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5lw9afvjsvj">[13]</a></sup></span><i>. </i>It can be shown that the standard deviation of this difference is&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Therefore, if we divide one by the other, the statistic&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will follow a normal distribution with unit variance and (under the null hypothesis) mean zero. We can then compare the observed value against tabulated values for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;to determine how likely it was to obtain such a result by chance<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2waz3ki93zr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2waz3ki93zr">[14]</a></sup></span>.</p><p>Below are plots showing how confidence in our conclusion grows with the number of observations, corresponding to the convergence plots above. The bold line shows the average across the p‑value experiments above. This is not exactly the true expected p‑value curve, but it already makes the point: achieving 95% confidence requires a <i>lot</i> of observations.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/xxvchlopizc4syqurb3h" /></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/s1jmtxnejmqw5hbljnqc" /></figure><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ycWWbpjxuhdxGpJ6e/tr7phqxbqmnralzmtlxc" /></figure><p>So what should we expect theoretically? We can "invert" the derivation above and compute how much data is needed to detect, with a given probability, an effect of known size d. Here are a few scenarios:</p><p>Theory says that to have 5% false positives and 5% false negatives, we need:</p><ul><li>For d = 0.3 - about 580 observations (i.e. roughly 290 before the intervention and 290 after; note how well this matches the separation point of the d‑value bundles above)</li><li>For d = 0.2 - about 1300 observations</li><li>For d = 0.1 - about 5200 (!) observations</li></ul><p>If we lower the bar slightly and allow 5% false positives and 20% false negatives, we need:</p><ul><li>For d = 0.3 - about 350 observations</li><li>For d = 0.2 - about 800 observations</li><li>For d = 0.1 - about 3150 observations</li></ul><p>If we lower the bar even further and allow 20% false positives and 20% false negatives:</p><ul><li>d = 0.3 - 200 observations</li><li>d = 0.2 - 250 observations</li><li>d = 0.1 - 1800 observations</li></ul><p>And finally, if we are absolutely certain that the effect must be positive and use a one‑sided test:</p><ul><li>d = 0.3 - 126 observations</li><li>d = 0.2 - 280 observations</li><li>d = 0.1 - 1120 observations</li></ul><p>So even if we significantly relax the confidence requirements and completely ignore the possibility that the intervention could do harm instead of good, detecting a not‑so‑weak effect of d = 0.3 still requires four months of observations! As a reminder, this is roughly the effect size of the first correctly chosen antidepressant for a patient with moderate depression. You can basically give up on trying to measure anything weaker. Vitamins (if you don’t have a specific deficiency), fish oil, and an extra 20 minutes of walking during the day (unless you’re usually completely sedentary) simply won’t be visible - even with very careful tracking.</p><h1>Additional complications</h1><p>But that’s still not all. There are several more effects worth paying attention to - each of them further increases the number of observations required.</p><h2>Non‑linear interactions</h2><p>It’s very rare for an intervention’s effect to be linear across the entire range we care about. Most interventions have a saturation threshold. The first extra hour of walking gives a larger effect than the third. This isn’t too bad, although it does mean we tend to underestimate the effect of <i>partial</i> interventions.</p><p>Much worse is when an intervention’s effect looks like an inverted U. The first cup of coffee makes you more alert, while the fifth makes you shake in hyper‑focused anxiety. Less than six hours of sleep is bad, but more than ten hours of sleep is also bad. Catching the optimal level of exposure is not always easy. Ideally, you’d want to test several intensity levels - which means running the experiment at least twice.</p><h2>Accumulation and time‑to‑effect</h2><p>Many interventions don’t produce an immediate effect. For example, atomoxetine (a medication for ADHD) has an effect ranging from d = 0.4 up to d = 2 (!) depending on the patient group and how symptoms are measured<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr50ggshkbph"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr50ggshkbph">[15]</a></sup></span>, but it can take up to four weeks to accumulate in the body. As a result, some ADHD patients may abandon even such an effective drug before it has time to work. The results of going to the gym or starting psychotherapy take even longer to show up.</p><p>Often, it’s not even clear what delay to expect. If you’re studying the relationship between weight loss and back pain, you probably shouldn’t expect the pain to go away immediately after losing weight. The damage to the back has already been done - it needs time to heal. But how long it takes for the effect to reach a plateau is unclear.</p><h2>Side conditions and seasonality</h2><p>The same intervention can be useful at one time and useless at another. Seasonality is the most obvious factor here, but it’s not the only one. An extra hour of walking or a vitamin D capsule may have a stronger effect in winter than in summer, because people tend to get less light and physical activity during the cold months. Sports supplements may work when you’re in a caloric surplus and do nothing in a deficit. Meditation might improve mood in the morning but harm sleep quality in the evening.</p><h2>Substitution effects</h2><p>Suppose you want to study how sleep deprivation affects you. You collect data on work productivity depending on whether you slept 6 or 8 hours. But if, after a six‑hour night, you drink more tea - and tea has a stimulating effect on you - you might not see any productivity drop from sleep deprivation in the data at all. You either need to fix all other parameters of the system (hard to do in practice), or collect data on them as well. Accounting for substitution effects complicates the model. And it’s often unclear in advance which factors might act as substitutes.</p><h2>"Noisy" measurement units</h2><p>If you’re measuring your own happiness or energy levels, you’ll run into consistency issues. People can usually distinguish between feeling 2/10 and 8/10 - but those extreme states don’t happen very often. Most of the time, you need to tell apart 4/10 from 5/10 and 5/10 from 6/10, and not everyone is that good at introspection. By the second month of a study, people get tired of doing this and start choosing between 4 and 5 at random. If there are no systematic biases, the estimate should still eventually converge to the "true" value. Still, measurement error adds on top of the error in the underlying state, increasing overall noise - and with it, the required number of measurements.</p><p>You should try to ground abstract target metrics either in questionnaires or in objective physiological indicators. But filling out questionnaires takes time, and it requires willpower not to start answering them carelessly. Objective physiological metrics have a different problem: thanks to wearable devices they’re easy to collect, but they’re usually only very indirectly related to what you actually care about. You can use resting heart‑rate variability as a proxy for energy or anxiety levels - but the connection is quite indirect.</p><h2>The observer effect</h2><p>It’s hard to run a blind experiment when you’re both the researcher and the subject. This only really works for pills - and even then, not everyone is willing to deal with the organizational hassle. You know what effect you’re hoping for, and you’ll subconsciously nudge your ratings in the desired direction.</p><p>Moreover, the very act of monitoring changes the system. Data collection makes you engage with everything more consciously. Mindfulness is generally a good thing, but it also creates a gap between the experimental setup and real life. Checklists add friction to activities that used to be automatic. Excessive attention to your own happiness and calmness can itself cause depression and anxiety. This effect persists even if you know about it.</p><h2>The general noise of life</h2><p>Work deadlines, unexpected trips, illnesses, and periods of unusually good mood will create "holes" in your data. You’ll need to decide which data points "count," or else log everything. You also need to decide what to do with missing data.</p><p>The best thing you can do for measurement quality is to reduce the overall noise level of the metric. Unfortunately, it’s very hard to put yourself into sufficiently controlled conditions for several months just to finish an experiment. You can try measuring more frequently, but beyond a certain (often unknown) threshold, increasing measurement frequency just collects more noise.</p><h1>Conclusion and silver lining</h1><p>"Data‑driven habit optimization" sounds cool and scientific, but in practice it’s a very situational endeavor. It’s worth attempting only if you:</p><ul><li>are confident that the intervention you’re studying has <i>at least</i> d = 0.3 (see the list of effects above; do you really expect your intervention to be comparable?)</li><li>can come up with ways to deal with the additional complications described in the previous section</li><li>are ready to dedicate several months to the study</li></ul><p>Estimate the expected cost of the data for such interventions. How much would you value information about the effect size? What is the expected cost of running the experiment? Even if this comparison suggests the study is worth doing, don’t expect an easy path or quick results. You will need <a href="https://gwern.net/nootropic/nootropics">this</a> amount of dedication to the science.</p><p>EDIT: I would like to clarify in response to the comments that I don't think that <i>any</i> data-driven life improvement is futile. My point that this particular flavor of rationalistic/nootrop-enthusiastic of searching and stacking d=0.1-0.3 improvements is very hard to implement. Other data-driven and generally goal-driven techniques are still very useful. For example:</p><ul><li>As I've mentioned, you don't know effect of certain intervention beforehand. In rare cases you may have a certain disorder you don't know about and which can be greately ameliorated. If, say, you have vitamin A deficiency and when you start taking vitamin A supplements you <i>notice</i> effect. In this case it will be relatively easy to prove that effect is not a placebo.<ul><li>So I guess, a fruitful strategy would be trying <i>many</i> things in sequence for a short period of time and investigating further if you notice any effect. Lossy but implementable.</li></ul></li><li>Sometimes the mere <i>noticing and paying attention</i> what's happening to you allows to you to thing if it's worth changing your life in some aspects.<ul><li>I've mentioned that sometimes the tracking itself introduces harmful friction into the process. However sometimes tracking itself helps! A common piece of advice in weightlifting is to track your progress. Same with losing weight.</li><li>It's hard to fit outliers into normal distribution but noticing outliers in data motivates you to think why those outliers happened. They give you more information about yourself, uncover unknown unknowns.</li><li>Sometimes seeing negative patterns in data allows you to react to problems before they happen (on the other hand, see also: hypochondria)</li></ul></li><li>Sometimes hard data help to gather your willpower to implement certain changes. For example when I do any intellectually demanding tasks after 22:00 my sleep quality drastically drops. I knew about that effect for some time but tried not to do anything at night only half-heartedly. Measuring that drop helped me to abstain from coding at night more consistently.</li><li>I've mentioned that it's hard to achieve meaningful effect size on 'happiness' and other broad metrics but on the flip side it's not that hard to achieve an effect on some narrow and easily measurable things. For example, tracking your blood sugar or blood pressure and expermenting on how different things affect them is much more reliable.</li><li>In rare cases experimenting triggers cascade effects or changes system around you enough that normal distribution is no longer applicable to modeling your situation.</li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item"
